But like I said, it doesn't matter if it's printed on the left margin, the right margin, or smack in the middle of the page—it just happens wherever my brain decides to take a little unannounced vacation.\n\nDoctor: When you're reading the newspaper or looking at your woodworking plans, do you ever find yourself naturally leaning or turning your body one way or the other without thinking about it—like your body just shifts in one direction while you're concentrating?\n\nPatient: Leaning? Hmm, no, I can't say that I do that. Actually, if you saw the stool I use out in the garage, you'd know right away why I don't lean. It’s one of those old-school metal shop stools, you know the kind? Just a round metal seat, no back to it whatsoever. I picked it up at a flea market over in Springfield... oh, gosh, it must have been ten, maybe fifteen years ago now. The guy wanted ten bucks for it, but I talked him down to five. Best five dollars I ever spent, honestly. But the thing is, it really forces you to sit up straight. If you start leaning to the left or the right on that thing while you're looking at your blueprints, you're going to end up flat on your back in a pile of sawdust! \n\nAnd it's the exact same deal inside at the kitchen table when I'm reading the Tribune. I've got these sturdy oak chairs that go with that table I told you I built back in '98. They have these high spindle backs—took me absolutely forever to turn all those spindles on the lathe, let me tell you, I thought I was going to go cross-eyed by the time I was done—but they support your back perfectly right down the middle. So I just sit there, square in the chair, both feet planted right on the linoleum. \n\nSo, no, when I'm trying to concentrate, I'm not shifting my weight around or tilting my body over to one side. I usually just sit perfectly still, practically staring a hole right through the paper. My body stays parked exactly where I put it. Like I was saying before, it's really just my train of thought that’s doing all the drifting and leaning, if you catch my drift. My brain goes completely sideways on me and I lose my place, but physically? No, I'm sitting straight as an arrow.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting at your kitchen table reading the newspaper, does your wife ever mention that you tend to turn your head to one side, or have you noticed that you naturally look more to your left or right when you're reading—even if it doesn't feel like you're doing it?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, as far as my wife goes... well, Mary actually passed away about four years ago this coming October. Bless her heart, we were together for almost forty years. But back when she was here, we had this whole morning routine, you know? I’d be sitting there with the main sections of the Tribune, and she’d always take the entertainment section because she loved doing the daily crossword puzzle. She’d use a pen, too—never a pencil, she was sharp as a tack like that. Anyway, she sat right across from me at that same oak table every single morning for decades, and she never once mentioned me turning my head to one side or looking at the paper sideways. \n\nAnd let me tell you, if I was doing something peculiar like that, Mary absolutely would have pointed it out. She wasn't shy about that sort of thing, not one bit. If I was chewing my toast too loud, or if I had a stray eyebrow hair getting too long, or if I put on a shirt that clashed with my trousers, she’d let me know right away. So if I was sitting there with my head cranked over to the left or the right like a dog listening to a strange noise, she would have stopped dead in her crossword and asked me what on earth was wrong with my neck. \n\nAnd as for me noticing it myself now that it's just me in the mornings? No, I really don't think I do that at all. Like we were talking about earlier, I just look straight ahead at whatever is in front of me. Now, my eyes might just sort of glaze over, sure. I might find myself staring dead ahead at the salt and pepper shakers for ten minutes trying to remember if I already took my morning vitamins, but my head is always pointed straight forward while I'm doing it. I'm just not doing any of this looking to the left or looking to the right business.\n\nDoctor: When you're reading that newspaper in the morning, if you look straight ahead at a word in the middle of the page, can you see the words to the left of it and the words to the right of it at the same time, or does one side seem to disappear from view?\n\nPatient: Disappear? No, no, nothing like that at all, doc. If I’m looking right smack in the middle of the page—let’s say I’m reading the sports section, checking the box scores to see how the Cubs did, even though they’ve been absolutely breaking my heart this season—if I focus on a word right in the center column, I can still see the columns on the left and the right perfectly fine. \n\nMy vision itself is actually pretty darn good for a guy my age. I mean, I wear these reading glasses, sure. I just picked them up at the pharmacy over on Elm Street. They're just your standard plus-two magnification, nothing fancy or prescription. I actually bought one of those three-packs they sell so I could leave one out in the garage by the workbench, one in the kitchen, and one on the nightstand... though, honestly, I still manage to misplace them half the time and end up walking around the house patting my shirt pockets looking for them. But anyway, as far as my actual field of vision goes? It’s all there. \n\nThe whole page is right there in front of me. The words on the left don't vanish into thin air, and the words on the right don't get blurry or disappear either. I can see the whole spread at the exact same time. The problem isn't that my eyes can't see the words on the sides, it's just that my brain doesn't always bother to process what I'm looking at. I'll be staring right at the whole page, seeing every single letter clearly from margin to margin, but the meaning just isn't sinking in. It’s like... well, it's like the camera is working perfectly fine, the lens is clean, but there’s absolutely no film in it, you know what I mean? So, no, nothing disappears from view at all. I see the whole thing, left to right, clear as day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

Oh, well, if you... if you held it right there? No, I’d see it plain as day, doc. It wouldn’t be invisible in the slightest. If I’m looking right smack at your nose right now, I can still see your shoulders, I can see that framed diploma you’ve got hanging on the wall over there to the left, I can see the doorknob... my peripheral vision is actually top-notch. 

In fact, I just had to renew my driver's license down at the DMV a couple of months ago. I still drive this old '85 Ford F-150—beautiful truck, two-tone blue and white. I rebuilt the carburetor on it myself back in... oh, it must have been 2012, right around the time they started putting all those new roundabouts in town, which I still say are a terrible idea for traffic flow, but nobody asked me. Anyway, they make you look into that little machine at the DMV and click the button when you see the flashing lights on the sides, you know the one? Passed it with flying colors. The gal working the counter even said I had the reaction time of a guy half my age. 

Which is a good thing, too, because you really need it driving down Route 9 these days. People are always flying up on your left side in the passing lane, usually staring down at their phones instead of the road, so you've got to be able to catch that movement out of the corner of your eye without taking your eyes off the car in front of you. If I couldn't see things on my left side, I would have been broadsided a dozen times by now, let me tell you. 

So, yeah, if you held a pencil out right where my left arm is, I’d definitely see it. I might not be able to read the brand name stamped on the side of it or tell you if it's a number two lead just from the corner of my eye, but I'd know exactly what it was and where you were holding it. Like I said, the physical equipment—the eyes, the vision—is all working exactly like it's supposed to. It's just my attention that decides to take a vacation on me!

Simple enough, right? But I ended up writing the completely wrong time down on the paper. I don't even know how I did it, my brain just sort of skimmed right past the actual numbers. \n\nAnd it’s not just there, it's at home, too. I do a little bit of tinkering, some woodworking in the garage, just small projects to pass the time. And I’ll have a piece of wood, and I’ll measure the board—I always tell myself to measure twice, cut once, like my old man used to say—but I’ll measure it once, think I've got it perfectly lined up, and then I go and cut it, and wouldn't you know it, it's a hair short. It's because I just skipped over the small print on the tape measure, or I didn't look close enough at the little tick marks. It’s just these constant, tiny mistakes that are driving me up the wall. It feels like I just can't lock in on the fine print of anything I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: When you're looking at something up close, like the tape measure or the class sheet, do your eyes feel like they're focusing properly, or do the details just seem blurry to you?\n\nPatient: Oh, well, that's a good question. You know, I did wonder about that myself for a while, especially since I'm getting up there in years. You start to think, \"Is it my eyes going bad?\" My wife, God rest her soul, she used to need reading glasses for everything, couldn't read a menu at a restaurant without them. But for me, no, it's not like that at all. My vision is actually pretty darn good for a guy my age. I had my eyes checked, oh, maybe about eight months ago, down at that little optometrist place next to the grocery store—the one with the big green sign, you know it? Anyway, the doctor there said my eyes were doing just fine. \n\nSo when I'm looking at the tape measure, or that sign-up sheet at the senior center, it's not that the numbers are blurry or fuzzy or anything like that. The lines are sharp, the numbers are clear as day. It’s more like... my brain just doesn't register them right. I'm looking right at the little lines on the tape measure, I see them perfectly, but it's like my mind just skips a beat or glosses over the exact spot I'm supposed to be paying attention to. It's not a vision problem, it's a paying-attention problem, if that makes any sense. I just can't seem to hold my focus on the exact detail, even though my eyes are doing their job just fine. It's incredibly frustrating, let me tell you, because if it was just my eyes, I could go buy a pair of cheap reading glasses at the pharmacy and be done with it! But it's not that simple.\n\nDoctor: Besides these attention problems with details, have you noticed any changes in your memory—like forgetting words, losing track of conversations, or having trouble remembering things you used to recall easily?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask about memory, because... well, I guess the short answer is no, not really. I mean, I don't feel like my memory is slipping away from me or anything like that. \n\nI know a lot of folks my age start worrying about that sort of thing. You hear all these stories, you know? Like my buddy Frank from the senior center—he's a couple years older than me, maybe 65 or 66—and he's always losing his keys, or he'll be right in the middle of telling you a story about his grandkids and he just completely blanks on the kid's name. And you can see him getting all flustered about it. But for me, I don't really have those moments. If I'm talking to somebody, I can hold my end of the conversation just fine. I don't find myself searching for words or forgetting what we were just talking about. \n\nAnd as far as remembering things from the past, or even just what I did yesterday, my mind is still pretty sharp in that department. I can tell you all about the cars I used to work on back in the 70s, or the exact layout of the house I grew up in. I remember to pay my bills on time, I remember when the trash needs to go out... well, most of the time anyway, unless I get distracted by something else in the garage. But it's not that I *forgot* the trash, it's just that my attention went somewhere else, you know what I mean? \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say my memory is going bad. I'm not losing words or losing track of conversations. It's really just this... this frustrating thing where I can't seem to lock onto the details right in front of me. Like I said with the tape measure, I remember *how* to measure, I remember *why* I'm measuring, I just mess up the actual looking-at-the-little-lines part. So the memory itself, the filing cabinet in my brain, that seems to be holding up okay for now.\n\nDoctor: When you're looking at something like the tape measure or that sign-up sheet, do you tend to miss details more on one side—like the left side or the right side—or does it happen pretty equally all over?\n\nPatient: Left side or right side? Hmm. You know, I’ve never really stopped to think about it quite like that, but... no, I’d have to say it’s definitely not a left or right kind of thing. It’s pretty much just all over the place. \n\nLike, if I’m holding my tape measure—I’ve got this old Stanley one, yellow tape, heavy metal case, the kind they don't really make anymore, you know? I’ve had it for probably twenty years. Anyway, when I pull that out to mark off, say, 18 and a quarter inches for a birdhouse I'm putting together, I’m looking straight down at the whole span of it. It’s not like the left side of the tape vanishes on me or I'm ignoring the right side of the board. I see the whole piece of wood, I see my pencil, I see the whole strip of numbers. It’s just that when I go to actually lock in on that specific little black line, my brain just sort of... slips, I guess, regardless of where it is on the tape. \n\nAnd it was the exact same deal with that sign-up sheet at the senior center. The paper was set up like a big grid, you know, with the days of the week across the top and the times going down the side. I didn't just miss the Monday column on the far left or the Friday column on the far right. I was looking right smack in the middle of the page, and I just wrote my name in the 10:00 AM slot instead of the 11:00 AM slot. They were right on top of each other! So it’s definitely not a directional thing, if that’s what you’re getting at. It’s more like a... a scattered thing. It just happens wherever it happens. \n\nActually, I was trying to figure out the exact same thing just the other day when I was sorting the mail on the kitchen counter, because I somehow managed to put my electric bill right into the junk mail pile. I was looking right at the stack, the whole pile was right in front of me, not off to one side, and I still just glossed right over the return address. So, yeah, it's pretty much equal opportunity when it comes to where I'm messing up the details.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on your woodworking projects or reading something, do you ever notice that you accidentally skip over words or numbers on one particular side—like always missing things on your left, or always on your right—even if you're not aware of it at the time?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, I think we might be circling around the same drain here, because like I was just saying a minute ago about the tape measure and the mail, it really isn't a left or right thing at all. It's funny you ask it again in a slightly different way, though, because it makes me think about when I'm reading the morning paper. \n\nI still get the physical paper delivered to the driveway every morning, you know, the local Tribune. My neighbor, Tom, he switched to reading everything on one of those tablets, but I just can't stand reading on a screen, it just doesn't feel right in your hands. Anyway, when I'm sitting there at the kitchen table with my coffee—black, no sugar, just how I've drank it for forty years—and I'm reading through the sports section or looking at the hardware store circulars, I might skip over a word here or there, or completely lose my place in a paragraph. But it's not like I'm only missing the words on the left margin, or completely ignoring the right side of the page. \n\nIt's just... I'm reading along, and suddenly my mind wanders off thinking about whether I need to buy more wood glue for that birdhouse I mentioned earlier, and I realize I just skimmed three whole sentences right in the dead center of the column without actually taking in a single word. Same with the woodworking. If I'm reading a set of plans for a project, I might misread a dimension, but it could be anywhere on the page. Left, right, top, bottom... it really doesn't matter. It's just a general skipping of the details, I guess. Not a blind spot on one side. I feel like I'd definitely notice if half the newspaper or half my workbench was just disappearing on me! So no, it's definitely not a left or right issue, just a paying-attention issue all over the place.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever noticed that you can't see things off to one side when you're looking straight ahead—like if someone walks up to you from your left or right, do you sometimes not notice them until they're right in front of you?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, you really are determined with this left and right business today! I feel like I'm taking a driving test all over again. But no, to answer your question, I don't have any trouble seeing things coming at me from the sides. My eyesight out of the corners of my eyes is perfectly fine. \n\nI actually spend a lot of my afternoons sitting out on my front porch. I've got this old wooden rocking chair—bought it at a flea market a couple counties over maybe fifteen years ago, stripped it completely down, re-stained it myself with a nice cherry finish. If Mrs. Gable's golden retriever gets loose—which happens at least twice a month because her teenage grandson always leaves their side gate unlatched when he takes the trash cans out—and it comes trotting up the sidewalk from my left, I spot him way down by the Johnson's driveway. Like I've been saying, my eyes work great, it's just my attention that decides to take a walk sometimes.\n\nDoctor: I'd like to do a quick test with you right now, if that's okay. It really does feel like I'm back at the DMV, though, like I was just saying. Like my buddy Frank from the senior center—he's a couple years older than me, maybe 65 or 66—and he's always losing his keys, or he'll be right in the middle of telling you a story about his grandkids and he just completely blanks on the kid's name. And you can see him getting all flustered about it. But for me, I don't really have those moments. If I'm talking to somebody, I can hold my end of the conversation just fine. I don't find myself searching for words or forgetting what we were just talking about. \n\nAnd as far as remembering things from the past, or even just what I did yesterday, my mind is still pretty sharp in that department. I can tell you all about the cars I used to work on back in the 70s, or the exact layout of the house I grew up in. I remember to pay my bills on time, I remember when the trash needs to go out... well, most of the time anyway, unless I get distracted by something else in the garage. But it's not that I *forgot* the trash, it's just that my attention went somewhere else, you know what I mean? \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say my memory is going bad. I'm not losing words or losing track of conversations. It's really just this... this frustrating thing where I can't seem to lock onto the details right in front of me. Like I said with the tape measure, I remember *how* to measure, I remember *why* I'm measuring, I just mess up the actual looking-at-the-little-lines part. So the memory itself, the filing cabinet in my brain, that seems to be holding up okay for now.\n\nDoctor: When you're looking at something like the tape measure or that sign-up sheet, do you tend to miss details more on one side—like the left side or the right side—or does it happen pretty equally all over?\n\nPatient: Left side or right side? Hmm. You know, I’ve never really stopped to think about it quite like that, but... no, I’d have to say it’s definitely not a left or right kind of thing. It’s pretty much just all over the place. \n\nLike, if I’m holding my tape measure—I’ve got this old Stanley one, yellow tape, heavy metal case, the kind they don't really make anymore, you know? I’ve had it for probably twenty years. Anyway, when I pull that out to mark off, say, 18 and a quarter inches for a birdhouse I'm putting together, I’m looking straight down at the whole span of it. It’s not like the left side of the tape vanishes on me or I'm ignoring the right side of the board. I see the whole piece of wood, I see my pencil, I see the whole strip of numbers. It’s just that when I go to actually lock in on that specific little black line, my brain just sort of... slips, I guess, regardless of where it is on the tape. \n\nAnd it was the exact same deal with that sign-up sheet at the senior center. The paper was set up like a big grid, you know, with the days of the week across the top and the times going down the side. I didn't just miss the Monday column on the far left or the Friday column on the far right. I was looking right smack in the middle of the page, and I just wrote my name in the 10:00 AM slot instead of the 11:00 AM slot. They were right on top of each other! So it’s definitely not a directional thing, if that’s what you’re getting at. It’s more like a... a scattered thing. It just happens wherever it happens. \n\nActually, I was trying to figure out the exact same thing just the other day when I was sorting the mail on the kitchen counter, because I somehow managed to put my electric bill right into the junk mail pile. I was looking right at the stack, the whole pile was right in front of me, not off to one side, and I still just glossed right over the return address. So, yeah, it's pretty much equal opportunity when it comes to where I'm messing up the details.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on your woodworking projects or reading something, do you ever notice that you accidentally skip over words or numbers on one particular side—like always missing things on your left, or always on your right—even if you're not aware of it at the time?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, I think we might be circling around the same drain here, because like I was just saying a minute ago about the tape measure and the mail, it really isn't a left or right thing at all. It's funny you ask it again in a slightly different way, though, because it makes me think about when I'm reading the morning paper. \n\nI still get the physical paper delivered to the driveway every morning, you know, the local Tribune. My neighbor, Tom, he switched to reading everything on one of those tablets, but I just can't stand reading on a screen, it just doesn't feel right in your hands. Anyway, when I'm sitting there at the kitchen table with my coffee—black, no sugar, just how I've drank it for forty years—and I'm reading through the sports section or looking at the hardware store circulars, I might skip over a word here or there, or completely lose my place in a paragraph. But it's not like I'm only missing the words on the left margin, or completely ignoring the right side of the page. \n\nIt's just... I'm reading along, and suddenly my mind wanders off thinking about whether I need to buy more wood glue for that birdhouse I mentioned earlier, and I realize I just skimmed three whole sentences right in the dead center of the column without actually taking in a single word. Same with the woodworking. If I'm reading a set of plans for a project, I might misread a dimension, but it could be anywhere on the page. Left, right, top, bottom... it really doesn't matter. It's just a general skipping of the details, I guess. Not a blind spot on one side. I feel like I'd definitely notice if half the newspaper or half my workbench was just disappearing on me! So no, it's definitely not a left or right issue, just a paying-attention issue all over the place.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever noticed that you can't see things off to one side when you're looking straight ahead—like if someone walks up to you from your left or right, do you sometimes not notice them until they're right in front of you?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, you really are determined with this left and right business today! I feel like I'm taking a driving test all over again. But no, to answer your question, I don't have any trouble seeing things coming at me from the sides. My eyesight out of the corners of my eyes is perfectly fine. \n\nI actually spend a lot of my afternoons sitting out on my front porch. I've got this old wooden rocking chair—bought it at a flea market a couple counties over maybe fifteen years ago, stripped it completely down, re-stained it myself with a nice cherry finish. It squeaks a little on the left runner, but it's comfortable. Anyway, I sit out there just watching the neighborhood, and I can see everything going on without even turning my head. If Mrs. Gable's golden retriever gets loose—which happens at least twice a month because her teenage grandson always leaves their side gate unlatched when he takes the trash cans out—and it comes trotting up the sidewalk from my left, I spot him way down by the Johnson's driveway. I'll be looking straight ahead at the big oak tree in my front yard, and I still catch that movement right off to the side. \n\nAnd it's the exact same thing if someone walks up on my right. The mailman, Gary, he always cuts across my lawn on the right side instead of using the paved walkway. It's not like he just materializes out of thin air right in front of my face like some kind of magic trick, you know? \n\nI even had my eyes checked for my driver's license renewal just last year, and they did that test where you put your forehead against the machine and the little lights flash on the far sides, and I hit the button every single time. Like I've been saying, my eyes work great, it's just my attention that decides to take a walk sometimes.\n\nDoctor: I'd like to do a quick test with you right now, if that's okay. It really does feel like I'm back at the DMV, though, like I was just saying. Shame about that store, they had the best deli counter, used to get my shaved ham there every Sunday. Coach told me to keep my eye on the ball, and boy did I! \n\nAnyway, I'm looking straight at it. I'm ready whenever you are. \n\n...Yep, there's the left side. \n...And the right. \n...Yep, both of them that time. \n...Left again. \n\nSee? I catch it the very second you even twitch a knuckle. It's just... well, like I said, if your fingers were a set of instructions for my woodworking, I might forget what I just looked at, but I definitely see them moving!\n\nDoctor: When you're reading that newspaper at the kitchen table, or looking at your woodworking plans, do you ever find that you have to turn your head or move the paper to see certain parts of it—like do you naturally shift things around to get a better look, even though you might not realize you're doing it?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, to be completely honest with you, no, I don't do anything like that. I don't need to turn my head like an owl or slide the paper back and forth like an old typewriter just to read the margins. \n\nWhen I'm sitting there at the kitchen table—it's a nice sturdy oak table, by the way, built it myself back in '98, seats six comfortably but usually it's just me and my coffee these days—I just lay the paper flat out right in front of me. I've got real good lighting in there, too. I actually just changed all the bulbs in the overhead fixture to those new daylight LEDs last month because the old yellow ones were making the room look dingy, you know? The words on the left don't vanish into thin air, and the words on the right don't get blurry or disappear either. I can see the whole spread at the exact same time. I'll be staring right at the whole page, seeing every single letter clearly from margin to margin, but the meaning just isn't sinking in. The words on the left don't vanish into thin air, and the words on the right don't get blurry or disappear either. I can see the whole spread at the exact same time. The problem isn't that my eyes can't see the words on the sides, it's just that my brain doesn't always bother to process what I'm looking at. I'll be staring right at the whole page, seeing every single letter clearly from margin to margin, but the meaning just isn't sinking in. It’s like... well, it's like the camera is working perfectly fine, the lens is clean, but there’s absolutely no film in it, you know what I mean? So, no, nothing disappears from view at all. If I’m looking right smack at your nose right now, I can still see your shoulders, I can see that framed diploma you’ve got hanging on the wall over there to the left, I can see the doorknob... my peripheral vision is actually top-notch. \n\nIn fact, I just had to renew my driver's license down at the DMV a couple of months ago. I still drive this old '85 Ford F-150—beautiful truck, two-tone blue and white. I rebuilt the carburetor on it myself back in... oh, it must have been 2012, right around the time they started putting all those new roundabouts in town, which I still say are a terrible idea for traffic flow, but nobody asked me. Anyway, they make you look into that little machine at the DMV and click the button when you see the flashing lights on the sides, you know the one? Passed it with flying colors. The gal working the counter even said I had the reaction time of a guy half my age. \n\nWhich is a good thing, too, because you really need it driving down Route 9 these days. People are always flying up on your left side in the passing lane, usually staring down at their phones instead of the road, so you've got to be able to catch that movement out of the corner of your eye without taking your eyes off the car in front of you. If I couldn't see things on my left side, I would have been broadsided a dozen times by now, let me tell you. \n\nSo, yeah, if you held a pencil out right where my left arm is, I’d definitely see it. It's because I just skipped over the small print on the tape measure, or I didn't look close enough at the little tick marks. It’s just these constant, tiny mistakes that are driving me up the wall. It feels like I just can't lock in on the fine print of anything I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: When you're looking at something up close, like the tape measure or the class sheet, do your eyes feel like they're focusing properly, or do the details just seem blurry to you?\n\nPatient: Oh, well, that's a good question. You know, I did wonder about that myself for a while, especially since I'm getting up there in years. You start to think, \"Is it my eyes going bad?\" My wife, God rest her soul, she used to need reading glasses for everything, couldn't read a menu at a restaurant without them. But for me, no, it's not like that at all. My vision is actually pretty darn good for a guy my age. I had my eyes checked, oh, maybe about eight months ago, down at that little optometrist place next to the grocery store—the one with the big green sign, you know it? I'm looking right at the little lines on the tape measure, I see them perfectly, but it's like my mind just skips a beat or glosses over the exact spot I'm supposed to be paying attention to. It's not a vision problem, it's a paying-attention problem, if that makes any sense. I just can't seem to hold my focus on the exact detail, even though my eyes are doing their job just fine. It's incredibly frustrating, let me tell you, because if it was just my eyes, I could go buy a pair of cheap reading glasses at the pharmacy and be done with it! But it's not that simple.\n\nDoctor: Besides these attention problems with details, have you noticed any changes in your memory—like forgetting words, losing track of conversations, or having trouble remembering things you used to recall easily?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask about memory, because... well, I guess the short answer is no, not really. I mean, I don't feel like my memory is slipping away from me or anything like that. \n\nI know a lot of folks my age start worrying about that sort of thing. You hear all these stories, you know? Like my buddy Frank from the senior center—he's a couple years older than me, maybe 65 or 66—and he's always losing his keys, or he'll be right in the middle of telling you a story about his grandkids and he just completely blanks on the kid's name. And you can see him getting all flustered about it. But for me, I don't really have those moments. If I'm talking to somebody, I can hold my end of the conversation just fine. So the memory itself, the filing cabinet in my brain, that seems to be holding up okay for now.\n\nDoctor: When you're looking at something like the tape measure or that sign-up sheet, do you tend to miss details more on one side—like the left side or the right side—or does it happen pretty equally all over?\n\nPatient: Left side or right side? I actually just changed all the bulbs in the overhead fixture to those new daylight LEDs last month because the old yellow ones were making the room look dingy, you know? Took me three solid weekends just to sand the top down smooth enough so it wouldn't scratch up my projects. Anyway, I roll the blueprints or the sketches out on that bench, put a couple of heavy tape measures or maybe a wood block on the corners so the paper doesn't roll right back up on me, and I just stand over it. I can see the whole entire layout at once. Top to bottom, left to right. \n\nNow, whether my brain actually registers the number I'm looking at, well, that's the real trick, isn't it? Like I might stare right at a note that says 'cut to 14 inches' and then I'll go to the saw and cut it at 16 inches because my mind was off wandering, thinking about what I'm going to make for lunch or if I remembered to put the recycling bin on the curb. But I definitely didn't have to tilt my head or shift the paper around to see the '14' in the first place. My eyes take the whole page in just fine without me having to do any kind of neck gymnastics to get a better look!\n\nDoctor: When you're reading that newspaper or looking at your woodworking plans, do you ever notice that you've skipped entire words or numbers in a row, or do you find yourself re-reading the same line because you realize you missed something—and if so, does that tend to happen more on one side of the page or scattered throughout?\n\nPatient: Oh, now you're really getting to the heart of the matter, doc. That right there—skipping over things and having to read them again—that hits the nail right on the head. And to answer your question about where it happens, it's definitely not just on the left or the right. It's scattered all over the place, just completely willy-nilly. \n\nHonestly, that's exactly what brings me in here to see you. I just keep messing up these little things, these tiny details that I really shouldn't be getting wrong. It's incredibly frustrating. Like, just the other week down at the senior center—I go down there on Tuesdays and Thursdays, mostly to shoot pool with a couple of the guys, Frank and Bill, though Frank's been out with a bad knee lately. Anyway, sometimes I sign up for the little classes and events they put on. I was looking right at the sign-up sheet for this local history thing, reading the whole page, and I ended up writing down the completely wrong time on my own calendar. I wrote down 1:00 instead of 1:30 because my eyes just skipped right over the half-hour part. I showed up thirty minutes early and had to just sit out in the lobby reading three-month-old magazines while they set up the folding chairs. \n\nAnd it’s the exact same story out in the garage with my woodworking. I'll be standing at that big heavy workbench I told you about, looking right at the plans, and I'll measure a board once—measure twice, cut once, that's what my dad always taught me, but sometimes I just measure the once—and I'll still end up cutting it a hair short. And it's all because I completely skipped over the small print on the plans. I'll read the big whole number and just completely gloss over the little fractions or the tiny notes next to it. \n\nSo then I'm standing there with a piece of expensive oak that's useless for what I need, and I have to go back to the bench, re-read the exact same line on the paper, and that's when I realize I missed a whole word or number. So yes, I'm definitely skipping things and having to read the same line two or three times before the whole picture actually registers in my head. But like I said, it doesn't matter if it's printed on the left margin, the right margin, or smack in the middle of the page—it just happens wherever my brain decides to take a little unannounced vacation.\n\nDoctor: When you're reading the newspaper or looking at your woodworking plans, do you ever find yourself naturally leaning or turning your body one way or the other without thinking about it—like your body just shifts in one direction while you're concentrating?\n\nPatient: Leaning? Hmm, no, I can't say that I do that. Actually, if you saw the stool I use out in the garage, you'd know right away why I don't lean. It’s one of those old-school metal shop stools, you know the kind? Just a round metal seat, no back to it whatsoever. I picked it up at a flea market over in Springfield... oh, gosh, it must have been ten, maybe fifteen years ago now. The guy wanted ten bucks for it, but I talked him down to five. Best five dollars I ever spent, honestly. But the thing is, it really forces you to sit up straight. If you start leaning to the left or the right on that thing while you're looking at your blueprints, you're going to end up flat on your back in a pile of sawdust! \n\nAnd it's the exact same deal inside at the kitchen table when I'm reading the Tribune. I've got these sturdy oak chairs that go with that table I told you I built back in '98. They have these high spindle backs—took me absolutely forever to turn all those spindles on the lathe, let me tell you, I thought I was going to go cross-eyed by the time I was done—but they support your back perfectly right down the middle. So I just sit there, square in the chair, both feet planted right on the linoleum. \n\nSo, no, when I'm trying to concentrate, I'm not shifting my weight around or tilting my body over to one side. I usually just sit perfectly still, practically staring a hole right through the paper. My body stays parked exactly where I put it. Like I was saying before, it's really just my train of thought that’s doing all the drifting and leaning, if you catch my drift. My brain goes completely sideways on me and I lose my place, but physically? No, I'm sitting straight as an arrow.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting at your kitchen table reading the newspaper, does your wife ever mention that you tend to turn your head to one side, or have you noticed that you naturally look more to your left or right when you're reading—even if it doesn't feel like you're doing it?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, as far as my wife goes... well, Mary actually passed away about four years ago this coming October. Bless her heart, we were together for almost forty years. But back when she was here, we had this whole morning routine, you know? I’d be sitting there with the main sections of the Tribune, and she’d always take the entertainment section because she loved doing the daily crossword puzzle. She’d use a pen, too—never a pencil, she was sharp as a tack like that. Anyway, she sat right across from me at that same oak table every single morning for decades, and she never once mentioned me turning my head to one side or looking at the paper sideways. \n\nAnd let me tell you, if I was doing something peculiar like that, Mary absolutely would have pointed it out. She wasn't shy about that sort of thing, not one bit. If I was chewing my toast too loud, or if I had a stray eyebrow hair getting too long, or if I put on a shirt that clashed with my trousers, she’d let me know right away. So if I was sitting there with my head cranked over to the left or the right like a dog listening to a strange noise, she would have stopped dead in her crossword and asked me what on earth was wrong with my neck. \n\nAnd as for me noticing it myself now that it's just me in the mornings? No, I really don't think I do that at all. Like we were talking about earlier, I just look straight ahead at whatever is in front of me. Now, my eyes might just sort of glaze over, sure. I might find myself staring dead ahead at the salt and pepper shakers for ten minutes trying to remember if I already took my morning vitamins, but my head is always pointed straight forward while I'm doing it. I'm just not doing any of this looking to the left or looking to the right business.\n\nDoctor: When you're reading that newspaper in the morning, if you look straight ahead at a word in the middle of the page, can you see the words to the left of it and the words to the right of it at the same time, or does one side seem to disappear from view?\n\nPatient: Disappear? No, no, nothing like that at all, doc. If I’m looking right smack in the middle of the page—let’s say I’m reading the sports section, checking the box scores to see how the Cubs did, even though they’ve been absolutely breaking my heart this season—if I focus on a word right in the center column, I can still see the columns on the left and the right perfectly fine. \n\nMy vision itself is actually pretty darn good for a guy my age. I mean, I wear these reading glasses, sure. I just picked them up at the pharmacy over on Elm Street. They're just your standard plus-two magnification, nothing fancy or prescription. I actually bought one of those three-packs they sell so I could leave one out in the garage by the workbench, one in the kitchen, and one on the nightstand... though, honestly, I still manage to misplace them half the time and end up walking around the house patting my shirt pockets looking for them. But anyway, as far as my actual field of vision goes? It’s all there. \n\nThe whole page is right there in front of me. The words on the left don't vanish into thin air, and the words on the right don't get blurry or disappear either. I can see the whole spread at the exact same time. The problem isn't that my eyes can't see the words on the sides, it's just that my brain doesn't always bother to process what I'm looking at. I'll be staring right at the whole page, seeing every single letter clearly from margin to margin, but the meaning just isn't sinking in. It’s like... well, it's like the camera is working perfectly fine, the lens is clean, but there’s absolutely no film in it, you know what I mean? So, no, nothing disappears from view at all. I see the whole thing, left to right, clear as day.\n\nDoctor: Let me ask you something specific: if I were to hold a pencil out to your left side, about where your left arm would be, and you kept your eyes looking straight ahead at my nose without moving your eyes, would you be able to see that pencil at all, or would it be completely invisible to you?\n\nPatient: Oh, well, if you... if you held it right there? No, I’d see it plain as day, doc. It wouldn’t be invisible in the slightest. If I’m looking right smack at your nose right now, I can still see your shoulders, I can see that framed diploma you’ve got hanging on the wall over there to the left, I can see the doorknob... my peripheral vision is actually top-notch. \n\nIn fact, I just had to renew my driver's license down at the DMV a couple of months ago. I still drive this old '85 Ford F-150—beautiful truck, two-tone blue and white. I rebuilt the carburetor on it myself back in... oh, it must have been 2012, right around the time they started putting all those new roundabouts in town, which I still say are a terrible idea for traffic flow, but nobody asked me. Anyway, they make you look into that little machine at the DMV and click the button when you see the flashing lights on the sides, you know the one? Passed it with flying colors. The gal working the counter even said I had the reaction time of a guy half my age. \n\nWhich is a good thing, too, because you really need it driving down Route 9 these days. People are always flying up on your left side in the passing lane, usually staring down at their phones instead of the road, so you've got to be able to catch that movement out of the corner of your eye without taking your eyes off the car in front of you. If I couldn't see things on my left side, I would have been broadsided a dozen times by now, let me tell you. \n\nSo, yeah, if you held a pencil out right where my left arm is, I’d definitely see it. I might not be able to read the brand name stamped on the side of it or tell you if it's a number two lead just from the corner of my eye, but I'd know exactly what it was and where you were holding it. Like I said, the physical equipment—the eyes, the vision—is all working exactly like it's supposed to. It's just my attention that decides to take a vacation on me!\n\nDoctor: I want to try that test we talked about now. Keep your eyes looking straight at my nose without moving them. I'm going to hold my hand out to your left side, about arm's length away, and slowly bring it toward the center. Tell me the exact moment you first see my hand appear—not when you think it should be there, but when you actually see it come into view.\n\nPatient: Okay, doc, I'm looking right at your nose. Just staring straight at it. Not moving my eyes one bit. I'm ready whenever you are. Just going to keep my eyes locked right here. \n\n...Oh! Right there. Yep, I see it right there. Honestly, it feels like I saw it almost the second you put your arm out. It was way out there on the edge, but I caught the movement right away. \n\nLike I was saying before, it's just like when I'm driving that old F-150. You've got to have that wide field of view, especially with the way these kids drive today. Just last week, I was coming back from the hardware store—needed some new washers for the kitchen faucet, the thing's been dripping like crazy and driving me up the wall—and this fella in a little red sports car came zipping up on my left side, right in my blind spot. If I hadn't caught the flash of red out of the corner of my eye, exactly like how I just saw your hand right now, I probably would have merged right into him. And you know how much a fender bender costs these days? The insurance premiums alone would put me in the poorhouse. \n\nSo, yeah, my peripheral vision is still sharp as a tack. I saw your hand way out there on the left, clear as a bell, almost as soon as you started moving it. No problem at all with seeing things on the sides, doc. None whatsoever.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like, just the other week down at the senior center—I go down there on Tuesdays and Thursdays, mostly to shoot pool with a couple of the guys, Frank and Bill, though Frank's been out with a bad knee lately. Anyway, sometimes I sign up for the little classes and events they put on. I was looking right at the sign-up sheet for this local history thing, reading the whole page, and I ended up writing down the completely wrong time on my own calendar. I wrote down 1:00 instead of 1:30 because my eyes just skipped right over the half-hour part. I showed up thirty minutes early and had to just sit out in the lobby reading three-month-old magazines while they set up the folding chairs. \n\nAnd it’s the exact same story out in the garage with my woodworking. I'll be standing at that big heavy workbench I told you about, looking right at the plans, and I'll measure a board once—measure twice, cut once, that's what my dad always taught me, but sometimes I just measure the once—and I'll still end up cutting it a hair short. And it's all because I completely skipped over the small print on the plans. I'll read the big whole number and just completely gloss over the little fractions or the tiny notes next to it. \n\nSo then I'm standing there with a piece of expensive oak that's useless for what I need, and I have to go back to the bench, re-read the exact same line on the paper, and that's when I realize I missed a whole word or number. So yes, I'm definitely skipping things and having to read the same line two or three times before the whole picture actually registers in my head. But like I said, it doesn't matter if it's printed on the left margin, the right margin, or smack in the middle of the page—it just happens wherever my brain decides to take a little unannounced vacation.\n\nDoctor: When you're reading the newspaper or looking at your woodworking plans, do you ever find yourself naturally leaning or turning your body one way or the other without thinking about it—like your body just shifts in one direction while you're concentrating?\n\nPatient: Leaning? Hmm, no, I can't say that I do that. Actually, if you saw the stool I use out in the garage, you'd know right away why I don't lean. It’s one of those old-school metal shop stools, you know the kind? Just a round metal seat, no back to it whatsoever. I picked it up at a flea market over in Springfield... oh, gosh, it must have been ten, maybe fifteen years ago now. The guy wanted ten bucks for it, but I talked him down to five. Best five dollars I ever spent, honestly. But the thing is, it really forces you to sit up straight. If you start leaning to the left or the right on that thing while you're looking at your blueprints, you're going to end up flat on your back in a pile of sawdust! \n\nAnd it's the exact same deal inside at the kitchen table when I'm reading the Tribune. I've got these sturdy oak chairs that go with that table I told you I built back in '98. They have these high spindle backs—took me absolutely forever to turn all those spindles on the lathe, let me tell you, I thought I was going to go cross-eyed by the time I was done—but they support your back perfectly right down the middle. So I just sit there, square in the chair, both feet planted right on the linoleum. \n\nSo, no, when I'm trying to concentrate, I'm not shifting my weight around or tilting my body over to one side. I usually just sit perfectly still, practically staring a hole right through the paper. My body stays parked exactly where I put it. Like I was saying before, it's really just my train of thought that’s doing all the drifting and leaning, if you catch my drift. My brain goes completely sideways on me and I lose my place, but physically? No, I'm sitting straight as an arrow.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting at your kitchen table reading the newspaper, does your wife ever mention that you tend to turn your head to one side, or have you noticed that you naturally look more to your left or right when you're reading—even if it doesn't feel like you're doing it?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, as far as my wife goes... well, Mary actually passed away about four years ago this coming October. Bless her heart, we were together for almost forty years. But back when she was here, we had this whole morning routine, you know? I’d be sitting there with the main sections of the Tribune, and she’d always take the entertainment section because she loved doing the daily crossword puzzle. She’d use a pen, too—never a pencil, she was sharp as a tack like that. Anyway, she sat right across from me at that same oak table every single morning for decades, and she never once mentioned me turning my head to one side or looking at the paper sideways. \n\nAnd let me tell you, if I was doing something peculiar like that, Mary absolutely would have pointed it out. She wasn't shy about that sort of thing, not one bit. If I was chewing my toast too loud, or if I had a stray eyebrow hair getting too long, or if I put on a shirt that clashed with my trousers, she’d let me know right away. So if I was sitting there with my head cranked over to the left or the right like a dog listening to a strange noise, she would have stopped dead in her crossword and asked me what on earth was wrong with my neck. \n\nAnd as for me noticing it myself now that it's just me in the mornings? No, I really don't think I do that at all. Like we were talking about earlier, I just look straight ahead at whatever is in front of me. Now, my eyes might just sort of glaze over, sure. I might find myself staring dead ahead at the salt and pepper shakers for ten minutes trying to remember if I already took my morning vitamins, but my head is always pointed straight forward while I'm doing it. I'm just not doing any of this looking to the left or looking to the right business.\n\nDoctor: When you're reading that newspaper in the morning, if you look straight ahead at a word in the middle of the page, can you see the words to the left of it and the words to the right of it at the same time, or does one side seem to disappear from view?\n\nPatient: Disappear? No, no, nothing like that at all, doc. If I’m looking right smack in the middle of the page—let’s say I’m reading the sports section, checking the box scores to see how the Cubs did, even though they’ve been absolutely breaking my heart this season—if I focus on a word right in the center column, I can still see the columns on the left and the right perfectly fine. \n\nMy vision itself is actually pretty darn good for a guy my age. I mean, I wear these reading glasses, sure. I just picked them up at the pharmacy over on Elm Street. They're just your standard plus-two magnification, nothing fancy or prescription. I actually bought one of those three-packs they sell so I could leave one out in the garage by the workbench, one in the kitchen, and one on the nightstand... though, honestly, I still manage to misplace them half the time and end up walking around the house patting my shirt pockets looking for them. But anyway, as far as my actual field of vision goes? It’s all there. \n\nThe whole page is right there in front of me. The words on the left don't vanish into thin air, and the words on the right don't get blurry or disappear either. I can see the whole spread at the exact same time. The problem isn't that my eyes can't see the words on the sides, it's just that my brain doesn't always bother to process what I'm looking at. I'll be staring right at the whole page, seeing every single letter clearly from margin to margin, but the meaning just isn't sinking in. It’s like... well, it's like the camera is working perfectly fine, the lens is clean, but there’s absolutely no film in it, you know what I mean? So, no, nothing disappears from view at all. I see the whole thing, left to right, clear as day.\n\nDoctor: Let me ask you something specific: if I were to hold a pencil out to your left side, about where your left arm would be, and you kept your eyes looking straight ahead at my nose without moving your eyes, would you be able to see that pencil at all, or would it be completely invisible to you?\n\nPatient: Oh, well, if you... if you held it right there? No, I’d see it plain as day, doc. It wouldn’t be invisible in the slightest. If I’m looking right smack at your nose right now, I can still see your shoulders, I can see that framed diploma you’ve got hanging on the wall over there to the left, I can see the doorknob... my peripheral vision is actually top-notch. \n\nIn fact, I just had to renew my driver's license down at the DMV a couple of months ago. I still drive this old '85 Ford F-150—beautiful truck, two-tone blue and white. I rebuilt the carburetor on it myself back in... oh, it must have been 2012, right around the time they started putting all those new roundabouts in town, which I still say are a terrible idea for traffic flow, but nobody asked me. Anyway, they make you look into that little machine at the DMV and click the button when you see the flashing lights on the sides, you know the one? Passed it with flying colors. The gal working the counter even said I had the reaction time of a guy half my age. \n\nWhich is a good thing, too, because you really need it driving down Route 9 these days. People are always flying up on your left side in the passing lane, usually staring down at their phones instead of the road, so you've got to be able to catch that movement out of the corner of your eye without taking your eyes off the car in front of you. If I couldn't see things on my left side, I would have been broadsided a dozen times by now, let me tell you. \n\nSo, yeah, if you held a pencil out right where my left arm is, I’d definitely see it. I might not be able to read the brand name stamped on the side of it or tell you if it's a number two lead just from the corner of my eye, but I'd know exactly what it was and where you were holding it. Like I said, the physical equipment—the eyes, the vision—is all working exactly like it's supposed to. It's just my attention that decides to take a vacation on me!\n\nDoctor: I want to try that test we talked about now. Keep your eyes looking straight at my nose without moving them. I'm going to hold my hand out to your left side, about arm's length away, and slowly bring it toward the center. Tell me the exact moment you first see my hand appear—not when you think it should be there, but when you actually see it come into view.\n\nPatient: Okay, doc, I'm looking right at your nose. Just staring straight at it. Not moving my eyes one bit. I'm ready whenever you are. Just going to keep my eyes locked right here. \n\n...Oh! Right there. Yep, I see it right there. Honestly, it feels like I saw it almost the second you put your arm out. It was way out there on the edge, but I caught the movement right away. \n\nLike I was saying before, it's just like when I'm driving that old F-150. You've got to have that wide field of view, especially with the way these kids drive today. Just last week, I was coming back from the hardware store—needed some new washers for the kitchen faucet, the thing's been dripping like crazy and driving me up the wall—and this fella in a little red sports car came zipping up on my left side, right in my blind spot. If I hadn't caught the flash of red out of the corner of my eye, exactly like how I just saw your hand right now, I probably would have merged right into him. And you know how much a fender bender costs these days? The insurance premiums alone would put me in the poorhouse. \n\nSo, yeah, my peripheral vision is still sharp as a tack. I saw your hand way out there on the left, clear as a bell, almost as soon as you started moving it. No problem at all with seeing things on the sides, doc. None whatsoever.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, like I was just saying, doc, it was way out there. Definitely quite far to the left. It wasn't anywhere near the middle of my body when I first noticed it. If I had to put a measurement on it... gosh, it felt like it was almost straight out from my shoulder. Like if you were standing directly beside me, right at nine o'clock. 

I could see the movement of your hand before you even really started bringing it inward. It's funny you ask so specifically about where it was, because my wife, Susan, she's always telling me I don't notice things right in front of my own nose. Like, if she leaves a grocery list on the kitchen island, or if I leave my keys right by the toaster, I'll walk past them three, maybe four times without seeing them. It drives her absolutely up the wall. But if a squirrel runs across the top of the fence way out in the far corner of the backyard while I'm sitting there drinking my morning coffee? I spot it instantly. Every single time. 

So, yeah, to answer your question, your hand was way, way out to the left. Not close to the middle at all. It was clear out on the very edge of where a person can possibly see, and I picked it up right away.

None whatsoever.\n\nDoctor: When I held my hand out to your left side just now and moved it toward the center, at what point exactly did you first see it—was it already quite far to the left, or was it much closer to the middle of your body before you noticed it?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying, doc, it was way out there. Definitely quite far to the left. It wasn't anywhere near the middle of my body when I first noticed it. It drives her absolutely up the wall. But if a squirrel runs across the top of the fence way out in the far corner of the backyard while I'm sitting there drinking my morning coffee?     "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
```json
{"candidates": ["H53.1", "G31.83", "F06.8"], "note": "Patient demonstrates clear left homonymous hemianopia on confrontation testing - hand was detected only when far to the left (at ~90 degrees), not in the true left visual field closer to center. This is inconsistent with patient's subjective report of good peripheral vision. Combined with attention/detail-processing difficulties and no memory impairment, this suggests a right occipital or parietal lesion causing visual field defect with associated neglect. H53.1 (homonymous vision loss) remains primary candidate. G31.83 (other specified frontotemporal dementia) less likely given preserved memory and specific visual field finding. F06.8 (other specified mental disorder due to known physiological condition) could apply if secondary to structural lesion.", "is_final": false}
```

No, no, nothing like that, doc. It didn't pop into view or suddenly appear out of thin air. I saw it gradually the whole entire time, right from the very start. It was just a smooth, continuous motion. 

Kind of like... well, you know when you're watching a plane come in for a landing? We used to live out by the county airport back in the late nineties, right off of Miller Road—nice little ranch house, though the noise could be a bit much on the weekends when they had the flight schools going—and you could sit out on the back porch and watch those little Cessnas come in. You didn't just suddenly see them when they were right over the house, you'd track them the whole way across the horizon. That's exactly how your hand was just now. 

I picked it up way out there on the left edge, and my peripheral vision just held onto it the whole time you were bringing it toward my nose. There wasn't any blind spot or a gap where it vanished and came back. It didn't jump or teleport, if that makes sense. Just a steady, gradual movement the whole way in. I tell you, my eyes are about the only thing that still works like a well-oiled machine. Now, if you want to talk about things popping and stuttering, you should hear my old lawnmower when I try to start it up on a chilly morning, but my vision? No, sir. Smooth as silk the whole way across. I saw your hand every single inch of the way.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nH53.1, G31.83, F06.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, and welcome. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, hello Doctor. Nice to meet you. I, uh, I guess what brings me in today is... well, it’s just been getting incredibly frustrating lately. I mean, I’m 62 now, I’m not working anymore, so you’d think I’d have all the time in the world to just slow down and get things right, you know? But I just keep messing up these little things. It’s always the details. \n\nLike, just the other week down at the senior center—I go there sometimes just to keep busy, you know, socialize a bit so I'm not just sitting around the house staring at the walls all day—and I was filling out this class sheet. Simple enough, right? But I ended up writing the completely wrong time down on the paper. I don't even know how I did it, my brain just sort of skimmed right past the actual numbers. \n\nAnd it’s not just there, it's at home, too. I do a little bit of tinkering, some woodworking in the garage, just small projects to pass the time. And I’ll have a piece of wood, and I’ll measure the board—I always tell myself to measure twice, cut once, like my old man used to say—but I’ll measure it once, think I've got it perfectly lined up, and then I go and cut it, and wouldn't you know it, it's a hair short. It's because I just skipped over the small print on the tape measure, or I didn't look close enough at the little tick marks. It’s just these constant, tiny mistakes that are driving me up the wall. It feels like I just can't lock in on the fine print of anything I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: When you're looking at something up close, like the tape measure or the class sheet, do your eyes feel like they're focusing properly, or do the details just seem blurry to you?\n\nPatient: Oh, well, that's a good question. You know, I did wonder about that myself for a while, especially since I'm getting up there in years. You start to think, \"Is it my eyes going bad?\" My wife, God rest her soul, she used to need reading glasses for everything, couldn't read a menu at a restaurant without them. But for me, no, it's not like that at all. My vision is actually pretty darn good for a guy my age. I had my eyes checked, oh, maybe about eight months ago, down at that little optometrist place next to the grocery store—the one with the big green sign, you know it? Anyway, the doctor there said my eyes were doing just fine. \n\nSo when I'm looking at the tape measure, or that sign-up sheet at the senior center, it's not that the numbers are blurry or fuzzy or anything like that. The lines are sharp, the numbers are clear as day. It’s more like... my brain just doesn't register them right. I'm looking right at the little lines on the tape measure, I see them perfectly, but it's like my mind just skips a beat or glosses over the exact spot I'm supposed to be paying attention to. It's not a vision problem, it's a paying-attention problem, if that makes any sense. I just can't seem to hold my focus on the exact detail, even though my eyes are doing their job just fine. It's incredibly frustrating, let me tell you, because if it was just my eyes, I could go buy a pair of cheap reading glasses at the pharmacy and be done with it! But it's not that simple.\n\nDoctor: Besides these attention problems with details, have you noticed any changes in your memory—like forgetting words, losing track of conversations, or having trouble remembering things you used to recall easily?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask about memory, because... well, I guess the short answer is no, not really. I mean, I don't feel like my memory is slipping away from me or anything like that. \n\nI know a lot of folks my age start worrying about that sort of thing. You hear all these stories, you know? Like my buddy Frank from the senior center—he's a couple years older than me, maybe 65 or 66—and he's always losing his keys, or he'll be right in the middle of telling you a story about his grandkids and he just completely blanks on the kid's name. And you can see him getting all flustered about it. But for me, I don't really have those moments. If I'm talking to somebody, I can hold my end of the conversation just fine. I don't find myself searching for words or forgetting what we were just talking about. \n\nAnd as far as remembering things from the past, or even just what I did yesterday, my mind is still pretty sharp in that department. I can tell you all about the cars I used to work on back in the 70s, or the exact layout of the house I grew up in. I remember to pay my bills on time, I remember when the trash needs to go out... well, most of the time anyway, unless I get distracted by something else in the garage. But it's not that I *forgot* the trash, it's just that my attention went somewhere else, you know what I mean? \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say my memory is going bad. I'm not losing words or losing track of conversations. It's really just this... this frustrating thing where I can't seem to lock onto the details right in front of me. Like I said with the tape measure, I remember *how* to measure, I remember *why* I'm measuring, I just mess up the actual looking-at-the-little-lines part. So the memory itself, the filing cabinet in my brain, that seems to be holding up okay for now.\n\nDoctor: When you're looking at something like the tape measure or that sign-up sheet, do you tend to miss details more on one side—like the left side or the right side—or does it happen pretty equally all over?\n\nPatient: Left side or right side? Hmm. You know, I’ve never really stopped to think about it quite like that, but... no, I’d have to say it’s definitely not a left or right kind of thing. It’s pretty much just all over the place. \n\nLike, if I’m holding my tape measure—I’ve got this old Stanley one, yellow tape, heavy metal case, the kind they don't really make anymore, you know? I’ve had it for probably twenty years. Anyway, when I pull that out to mark off, say, 18 and a quarter inches for a birdhouse I'm putting together, I’m looking straight down at the whole span of it. It’s not like the left side of the tape vanishes on me or I'm ignoring the right side of the board. I see the whole piece of wood, I see my pencil, I see the whole strip of numbers. It’s just that when I go to actually lock in on that specific little black line, my brain just sort of... slips, I guess, regardless of where it is on the tape. \n\nAnd it was the exact same deal with that sign-up sheet at the senior center. The paper was set up like a big grid, you know, with the days of the week across the top and the times going down the side. I didn't just miss the Monday column on the far left or the Friday column on the far right. I was looking right smack in the middle of the page, and I just wrote my name in the 10:00 AM slot instead of the 11:00 AM slot. They were right on top of each other! So it’s definitely not a directional thing, if that’s what you’re getting at. It’s more like a... a scattered thing. It just happens wherever it happens. \n\nActually, I was trying to figure out the exact same thing just the other day when I was sorting the mail on the kitchen counter, because I somehow managed to put my electric bill right into the junk mail pile. I was looking right at the stack, the whole pile was right in front of me, not off to one side, and I still just glossed right over the return address. So, yeah, it's pretty much equal opportunity when it comes to where I'm messing up the details.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on your woodworking projects or reading something, do you ever notice that you accidentally skip over words or numbers on one particular side—like always missing things on your left, or always on your right—even if you're not aware of it at the time?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, I think we might be circling around the same drain here, because like I was just saying a minute ago about the tape measure and the mail, it really isn't a left or right thing at all. It's funny you ask it again in a slightly different way, though, because it makes me think about when I'm reading the morning paper. \n\nI still get the physical paper delivered to the driveway every morning, you know, the local Tribune. My neighbor, Tom, he switched to reading everything on one of those tablets, but I just can't stand reading on a screen, it just doesn't feel right in your hands. Anyway, when I'm sitting there at the kitchen table with my coffee—black, no sugar, just how I've drank it for forty years—and I'm reading through the sports section or looking at the hardware store circulars, I might skip over a word here or there, or completely lose my place in a paragraph. But it's not like I'm only missing the words on the left margin, or completely ignoring the right side of the page. \n\nIt's just... I'm reading along, and suddenly my mind wanders off thinking about whether I need to buy more wood glue for that birdhouse I mentioned earlier, and I realize I just skimmed three whole sentences right in the dead center of the column without actually taking in a single word. Same with the woodworking. If I'm reading a set of plans for a project, I might misread a dimension, but it could be anywhere on the page. Left, right, top, bottom... it really doesn't matter. It's just a general skipping of the details, I guess. Not a blind spot on one side. I feel like I'd definitely notice if half the newspaper or half my workbench was just disappearing on me! So no, it's definitely not a left or right issue, just a paying-attention issue all over the place.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever noticed that you can't see things off to one side when you're looking straight ahead—like if someone walks up to you from your left or right, do you sometimes not notice them until they're right in front of you?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, you really are determined with this left and right business today! I feel like I'm taking a driving test all over again. But no, to answer your question, I don't have any trouble seeing things coming at me from the sides. My eyesight out of the corners of my eyes is perfectly fine. \n\nI actually spend a lot of my afternoons sitting out on my front porch. I've got this old wooden rocking chair—bought it at a flea market a couple counties over maybe fifteen years ago, stripped it completely down, re-stained it myself with a nice cherry finish. It squeaks a little on the left runner, but it's comfortable. Anyway, I sit out there just watching the neighborhood, and I can see everything going on without even turning my head. If Mrs. Gable's golden retriever gets loose—which happens at least twice a month because her teenage grandson always leaves their side gate unlatched when he takes the trash cans out—and it comes trotting up the sidewalk from my left, I spot him way down by the Johnson's driveway. I'll be looking straight ahead at the big oak tree in my front yard, and I still catch that movement right off to the side. \n\nAnd it's the exact same thing if someone walks up on my right. The mailman, Gary, he always cuts across my lawn on the right side instead of using the paved walkway. But I always see him coming out of the corner of my eye. It's not like he just materializes out of thin air right in front of my face like some kind of magic trick, you know? \n\nI even had my eyes checked for my driver's license renewal just last year, and they did that test where you put your forehead against the machine and the little lights flash on the far sides, and I hit the button every single time. So no, I definitely don't have any blind spots on the sides. Like I've been saying, my eyes work great, it's just my attention that decides to take a walk sometimes.\n\nDoctor: I'd like to do a quick test with you right now, if that's okay. I'm going to hold up my hands on either side of your head while you look straight ahead at my nose, and I'll wiggle my fingers. I want you to tell me the moment you see any movement, even in your peripheral vision. Ready?\n\nPatient: Oh, sure, sure, we can do that. I don't mind at all. It really does feel like I'm back at the DMV, though, like I was just saying. Shame about that store, they had the best deli counter, used to get my shaved ham there every Sunday. Always makes me feel like I'm playing an arcade game or something. But your way works too! No expensive machines needed, right? Just good old-fashioned hands. \n\nAlright, let me just adjust my glasses here so they're sitting right... okay. I am looking right smack at your nose. Honestly, you've got a very straight nose, doc, not to be weird about it. Mine's been a little crooked ever since I caught a baseball with my face back in high school. Coach told me to keep my eye on the ball, and boy did I! \n\nAnyway, I'm looking straight at it. I'm ready whenever you are. \n\n...Yep, there's the left side. \n...And the right. \n...Yep, both of them that time. \n...Left again. \n\nSee? I catch it the very second you even twitch a knuckle. Clear as day. Like I was telling you before, the peripheral vision is sharp as a tack. I can see everything going on out there on the edges. It's just... well, like I said, if your fingers were a set of instructions for my woodworking, I might forget what I just looked at, but I definitely see them moving!\n\nDoctor: When you're reading that newspaper at the kitchen table, or looking at your woodworking plans, do you ever find that you have to turn your head or move the paper to see certain parts of it—like do you naturally shift things around to get a better look, even though you might not realize you're doing it?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, to be completely honest with you, no, I don't do anything like that. I don't need to turn my head like an owl or slide the paper back and forth like an old typewriter just to read the margins. \n\nWhen I'm sitting there at the kitchen table—it's a nice sturdy oak table, by the way, built it myself back in '98, seats six comfortably but usually it's just me and my coffee these days—I just lay the paper flat out right in front of me. I've got real good lighting in there, too. I actually just changed all the bulbs in the overhead fixture to those new daylight LEDs last month because the old yellow ones were making the room look dingy, you know? So I just lay the Tribune down, and I can see the whole two-page spread perfectly fine without moving a muscle in my neck. \n\nAnd it's the exact same story with the woodworking plans out in the garage.
