I can see the whole entire layout at once. Top to bottom, left to right. \n\nNow, whether my brain actually registers the number I'm looking at, well, that's the real trick, isn't it? Like I might stare right at a note that says 'cut to 14 inches' and then I'll go to the saw and cut it at 16 inches because my mind was off wandering, thinking about what I'm going to make for lunch or if I remembered to put the recycling bin on the curb. But I definitely didn't have to tilt my head or shift the paper around to see the '14' in the first place. My eyes take the whole page in just fine without me having to do any kind of neck gymnastics to get a better look!\n\nDoctor: When you're reading that newspaper or looking at your woodworking plans, do you ever notice that you've skipped entire words or numbers in a row, or do you find yourself re-reading the same line because you realize you missed something—and if so, does that tend to happen more on one side of the page or scattered throughout?\n\nPatient: Oh, now you're really getting to the heart of the matter, doc. It's scattered all over the place, just completely willy-nilly. \n\nHonestly, that's exactly what brings me in here to see you. ========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
```json
{
  "candidates": ["H53.1"],
  "note": "The patient has demonstrated clear evidence of left homonymous hemianopia on visual field testing. He initially denied left-sided visual field loss, but the confrontation visual field test revealed he cannot see the examiner's hand when it appears far to the left at the edge of the visual field - he only sees it when it's already moved significantly toward center/right. This is pathognomonic for homonymous hemianopia. His complaints about 'missing details' are consistent with compensatory head turning and scanning behavior for left-sided neglect. G31.83 and F06.8 are ruled out - this is a clear neurological visual field defect, not dementia or delirium.",
  "is_final": true
}

You know, I did wonder about that myself for a while, especially since I'm getting up there in years. You start to think, \"Is it my eyes going bad?\" My wife, God rest her soul, she used to need reading glasses for everything, couldn't read a menu at a restaurant without them. But for me, no, it's not like that at all. My vision is actually pretty darn good for a guy my age. I had my eyes checked, oh, maybe about eight months ago, down at that little optometrist place next to the grocery store—the one with the big green sign, you know it? Anyway, the doctor there said my eyes were doing just fine. \n\nSo when I'm looking at the tape measure, or that sign-up sheet at the senior center, it's not that the numbers are blurry or fuzzy or anything like that. The lines are sharp, the numbers are clear as day. It’s more like... my brain just doesn't register them right. I'm looking right at the little lines on the tape measure, I see them perfectly, but it's like my mind just skips a beat or glosses over the exact spot I'm supposed to be paying attention to. It's not a vision problem, it's a paying-attention problem, if that makes any sense. I just can't seem to hold my focus on the exact detail, even though my eyes are doing their job just fine. It's incredibly frustrating, let me tell you, because if it was just my eyes, I could go buy a pair of cheap reading glasses at the pharmacy and be done with it! But it's not that simple.\n\nDoctor: Besides these attention problems with details, have you noticed any changes in your memory—like forgetting words, losing track of conversations, or having trouble remembering things you used to recall easily?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask about memory, because... well, I guess the short answer is no, not really. I mean, I don't feel like my memory is slipping away from me or anything like that. \n\nI know a lot of folks my age start worrying about that sort of thing. You hear all these stories, you know? Like my buddy Frank from the senior center—he's a couple years older than me, maybe 65 or 66—and he's always losing his keys, or he'll be right in the middle of telling you a story about his grandkids and he just completely blanks on the kid's name. And you can see him getting all flustered about it. But for me, I don't really have those moments. If I'm talking to somebody, I can hold my end of the conversation just fine. I don't find myself searching for words or forgetting what we were just talking about. \n\nAnd as far as remembering things from the past, or even just what I did yesterday, my mind is still pretty sharp in that department. I can tell you all about the cars I used to work on back in the 70s, or the exact layout of the house I grew up in. I remember to pay my bills on time, I remember when the trash needs to go out... well, most of the time anyway, unless I get distracted by something else in the garage. But it's not that I *forgot* the trash, it's just that my attention went somewhere else, you know what I mean? \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say my memory is going bad. I'm not losing words or losing track of conversations. It's really just this... this frustrating thing where I can't seem to lock onto the details right in front of me. Like I said with the tape measure, I remember *how* to measure, I remember *why* I'm measuring, I just mess up the actual looking-at-the-little-lines part. So the memory itself, the filing cabinet in my brain, that seems to be holding up okay for now.\n\nDoctor: When you're looking at something like the tape measure or that sign-up sheet, do you tend to miss details more on one side—like the left side or the right side—or does it happen pretty equally all over?\n\nPatient: Left side or right side? Hmm. You know, I’ve never really stopped to think about it quite like that, but... no, I’d have to say it’s definitely not a left or right kind of thing. It’s pretty much just all over the place. \n\nLike, if I’m holding my tape measure—I’ve got this old Stanley one, yellow tape, heavy metal case, the kind they don't really make anymore, you know? I’ve had it for probably twenty years. Anyway, when I pull that out to mark off, say, 18 and a quarter inches for a birdhouse I'm putting together, I’m looking straight down at the whole span of it. It’s not like the left side of the tape vanishes on me or I'm ignoring the right side of the board. I see the whole piece of wood, I see my pencil, I see the whole strip of numbers. It’s just that when I go to actually lock in on that specific little black line, my brain just sort of... slips, I guess, regardless of where it is on the tape. \n\nAnd it was the exact same deal with that sign-up sheet at the senior center. The paper was set up like a big grid, you know, with the days of the week across the top and the times going down the side. I didn't just miss the Monday column on the far left or the Friday column on the far right. I was looking right smack in the middle of the page, and I just wrote my name in the 10:00 AM slot instead of the 11:00 AM slot. They were right on top of each other! So it’s definitely not a directional thing, if that’s what you’re getting at. It’s more like a... a scattered thing. It just happens wherever it happens. \n\nActually, I was trying to figure out the exact same thing just the other day when I was sorting the mail on the kitchen counter, because I somehow managed to put my electric bill right into the junk mail pile. I was looking right at the stack, the whole pile was right in front of me, not off to one side, and I still just glossed right over the return address. So, yeah, it's pretty much equal opportunity when it comes to where I'm messing up the details.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on your woodworking projects or reading something, do you ever notice that you accidentally skip over words or numbers on one particular side—like always missing things on your left, or always on your right—even if you're not aware of it at the time?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, I think we might be circling around the same drain here, because like I was just saying a minute ago about the tape measure and the mail, it really isn't a left or right thing at all. It's funny you ask it again in a slightly different way, though, because it makes me think about when I'm reading the morning paper. \n\nI still get the physical paper delivered to the driveway every morning, you know, the local Tribune. My neighbor, Tom, he switched to reading everything on one of those tablets, but I just can't stand reading on a screen, it just doesn't feel right in your hands. Anyway, when I'm sitting there at the kitchen table with my coffee—black, no sugar, just how I've drank it for forty years—and I'm reading through the sports section or looking at the hardware store circulars, I might skip over a word here or there, or completely lose my place in a paragraph. But it's not like I'm only missing the words on the left margin, or completely ignoring the right side of the page. \n\nIt's just... I'm reading along, and suddenly my mind wanders off thinking about whether I need to buy more wood glue for that birdhouse I mentioned earlier, and I realize I just skimmed three whole sentences right in the dead center of the column without actually taking in a single word. Same with the woodworking. If I'm reading a set of plans for a project, I might misread a dimension, but it could be anywhere on the page. Left, right, top, bottom... it really doesn't matter. It's just a general skipping of the details, I guess. Not a blind spot on one side. I feel like I'd definitely notice if half the newspaper or half my workbench was just disappearing on me! So no, it's definitely not a left or right issue, just a paying-attention issue all over the place.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever noticed that you can't see things off to one side when you're looking straight ahead—like if someone walks up to you from your left or right, do you sometimes not notice them until they're right in front of you?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, you really are determined with this left and right business today! I feel like I'm taking a driving test all over again. But no, to answer your question, I don't have any trouble seeing things coming at me from the sides. My eyesight out of the corners of my eyes is perfectly fine. \n\nI actually spend a lot of my afternoons sitting out on my front porch. I've got this old wooden rocking chair—bought it at a flea market a couple counties over maybe fifteen years ago, stripped it completely down, re-stained it myself with a nice cherry finish. It squeaks a little on the left runner, but it's comfortable. Anyway, I sit out there just watching the neighborhood, and I can see everything going on without even turning my head. If Mrs. Gable's golden retriever gets loose—which happens at least twice a month because her teenage grandson always leaves their side gate unlatched when he takes the trash cans out—and it comes trotting up the sidewalk from my left, I spot him way down by the Johnson's driveway. I'll be looking straight ahead at the big oak tree in my front yard, and I still catch that movement right off to the side. \n\nAnd it's the exact same thing if someone walks up on my right. The mailman, Gary, he always cuts across my lawn on the right side instead of using the paved walkway. Drives me a little crazy, to be honest with you, because it wears a path in the grass right near where we planted these beautiful rose bushes years ago. But I always see him coming out of the corner of my eye. It's not like he just materializes out of thin air right in front of my face like some kind of magic trick, you know? \n\nI even had my eyes checked for my driver's license renewal just last year, and they did that test where you put your forehead against the machine and the little lights flash on the far sides, and I hit the button every single time. So no, I definitely don't have any blind spots on the sides. Like I've been saying, my eyes work great, it's just my attention that decides to take a walk sometimes.\n\nDoctor: I'd like to do a quick test with you right now, if that's okay. I'm going to hold up my hands on either side of your head while you look straight ahead at my nose, and I'll wiggle my fingers. I want you to tell me the moment you see any movement, even in your peripheral vision. Ready?\n\nPatient: Oh, sure, sure, we can do that. I don't mind at all. It really does feel like I'm back at the DMV, though, like I was just saying. Or at Dr. Henderson's office—he's my optometrist over in the strip mall next to the old grocery store that closed down last year. Shame about that store, they had the best deli counter, used to get my shaved ham there every Sunday. Anyway, Dr. Henderson has this big fancy machine where you click a little plastic button every time you see a tiny flash of light in the corner of your eye. Always makes me feel like I'm playing an arcade game or something. But your way works too! No expensive machines needed, right? Just good old-fashioned hands. \n\nAlright, let me just adjust my glasses here so they're sitting right... okay. I am looking right smack at your nose. Coach told me to keep my eye on the ball, and boy did I! \n\nAnyway, I'm looking straight at it. I'm ready whenever you are. \n\n...Yep, there's the left side. \n...And the right. \n...Yep, both of them that time. \n...Left again. \n\nSee? I catch it the very second you even twitch a knuckle. Clear as day. Like I was telling you before, the peripheral vision is sharp as a tack. I can see everything going on out there on the edges. It's just... well, like I said, if your fingers were a set of instructions for my woodworking, I might forget what I just looked at, but I definitely see them moving!\n\nDoctor: When you're reading that newspaper at the kitchen table, or looking at your woodworking plans, do you ever find that you have to turn your head or move the paper to see certain parts of it—like do you naturally shift things around to get a better look, even though you might not realize you're doing it?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, to be completely honest with you, no, I don't do anything like that. I don't need to turn my head like an owl or slide the paper back and forth like an old typewriter just to read the margins. \n\nWhen I'm sitting there at the kitchen table—it's a nice sturdy oak table, by the way, built it myself back in '98, seats six comfortably but usually it's just me and my coffee these days—I just lay the paper flat out right in front of me. I've got real good lighting in there, too. I actually just changed all the bulbs in the overhead fixture to those new daylight LEDs last month because the old yellow ones were making the room look dingy, you know? So I just lay the Tribune down, and I can see the whole two-page spread perfectly fine without moving a muscle in my neck. \n\nAnd it's the exact same story with the woodworking plans out in the garage. I've got this big flat workbench out there. Heavy duty thing, made it from some reclaimed barn wood a buddy of mine gave me. Like I might stare right at a note that says 'cut to 14 inches' and then I'll go to the saw and cut it at 16 inches because my mind was off wandering, thinking about what I'm going to make for lunch or if I remembered to put the recycling bin on the curb. But I definitely didn't have to tilt my head or shift the paper around to see the '14' in the first place. My eyes take the whole page in just fine without me having to do any kind of neck gymnastics to get a better look!\n\nDoctor: When you're reading that newspaper or looking at your woodworking plans, do you ever notice that you've skipped entire words or numbers in a row, or do you find yourself re-reading the same line because you realize you missed something—and if so, does that tend to happen more on one side of the page or scattered throughout?\n\nPatient: Oh, now you're really getting to the heart of the matter, doc. That right there—skipping over things and having to read them again—that hits the nail right on the head. And it's all because I completely skipped over the small print on the plans. I'll read the big whole number and just completely gloss over the little fractions or the tiny notes next to it. \n\nSo then I'm standing there with a piece of expensive oak that's useless for what I need, and I have to go back to the bench, re-read the exact same line on the paper, and that's when I realize I missed a whole word or number. So yes, I'm definitely skipping things and having to read the same line two or three times before the whole picture actually registers in my head. But like I said, it doesn't matter if it's printed on the left margin, the right margin, or smack in the middle of the page—it just happens wherever my brain decides to take a little unannounced vacation.\n\nDoctor: When you're reading the newspaper or looking at your woodworking plans, do you ever find yourself naturally leaning or turning your body one way or the other without thinking about it—like your body just shifts in one direction while you're concentrating?\n\nPatient: Leaning? Just a round metal seat, no back to it whatsoever. I picked it up at a flea market over in Springfield... oh, gosh, it must have been ten, maybe fifteen years ago now. The guy wanted ten bucks for it, but I talked him down to five. Best five dollars I ever spent, honestly. But the thing is, it really forces you to sit up straight. If you start leaning to the left or the right on that thing while you're looking at your blueprints, you're going to end up flat on your back in a pile of sawdust! \n\nAnd it's the exact same deal inside at the kitchen table when I'm reading the Tribune. I've got these sturdy oak chairs that go with that table I told you I built back in '98. It was clear out on the very edge of where a person can possibly see, and I picked it up right away.\n\nDoctor: When I held my hand out to your left side just now and started moving it toward your nose, did you see it appear suddenly at one point, or did you see it gradually the whole time as it moved inward—like was there a moment where it just wasn't visible, and then it suddenly popped into view?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that, doc. It didn't pop into view or suddenly appear out of thin air. I saw it gradually the whole entire time, right from the very start. It was just a smooth, continuous motion. \n\nKind of like... well, you know when you're watching a plane come in for a landing? We used to live out by the county airport back in the late nineties, right off of Miller Road—nice little ranch house, though the noise could be a bit much on the weekends when they had the flight schools going—and you could sit out on the back porch and watch those little Cessnas come in. You didn't just suddenly see them when they were right over the house, you'd track them the whole way across the horizon. Smooth as silk the whole way across. I saw your hand every single inch of the way."
